arXiv:2308.00226v2 [math.CO] 22 Jan 2025




Action convergence of general hypergraphs and tensors



Giulio Zucal
††thanks: giulio.zucal@mis.mpg.de

Affiliation: Max Planck Institute for Mathematics in the Sciences, Leipzig, Germany


August 24, 2026


Abstract
    

Action convergence provides a limit theory for linear bounded operators  A_{n}:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{n}) An:L∞​(Ωn)⟶L1​(Ωn)A_{n}:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{n}) where  \Omega_{n} Ωn\Omega_{n} are potentially different probability spaces. This notion of convergence emerged in graph limits theory as it unifies and generalizes many notions of graph limits. We generalize the theory of action convergence to sequences of multi-linear bounded operators  A_{n}:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\times\ldots\times L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{n}) An:L∞​(Ωn)×…×L∞​(Ωn)⟶L1​(Ωn)A_{n}:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\times\ldots\times L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{n}). Similarly to the linear case, we obtain that for a uniformly bounded (under an appropriate norm) sequence of multi-linear operators, there exists an action convergent subsequence. Additionally, we explain how to associate different types of multi-linear operators to a tensor and we study the different notions of convergence that we obtain for tensors and in particular for adjacency tensors of hypergraphs. We obtain several hypergraphs convergence notions and we link these with the hierarchy of notions of quasirandomness for hypergraph sequences. This convergence also covers sparse and inhomogeneous hypergraph sequences and it preserves many properties of adjacency tensors of hypergraphs. Moreover, we explain how to obtain a meaningful convergence for sequences of non-uniform hypergraphs and, therefore, also for simplicial complexes. Additionally, we highlight many connections with the theory of dense uniform hypergraph limits (hypergraphons) and we conjecture the equivalence of this theory with a modification of multi-linear action convergence.
    

Keywords: Graph limits, Hypergraphs, Action convergence, Tensors, Higher-order interactions
    

Mathematics Subject Classification Number: 05C65
  




1 Introduction




In the last 20 years, the study of complex networks has permeated many areas of social and natural sciences. Important examples are computer, telecommunication, biological, cognitive, semantic and social networks. In particular, in all of these areas, understanding large networks is a fundamental problem.




Network structures are usually modelled using graph theory to represent pairwise interactions between the elements of the network. However, for very large networks, such as the internet, the brain and social networks among others, exact information about the number of nodes and other specific features of the underlying graph is not available. For this reason, there is the need for a mathematical definition of synthetic structures containing only the relevant information for a very large graph. This is equivalent to assuming that the number of nodes is so big that the graph can be well approximated with a “graph-like” object with infinite number of nodes. This motivated the development of graph limits theory, the study of graph sequences, their convergence and their limit objects. In mathematical terms, one is interested in finding a metric on the space of graphs and a completion of this space with respect to this metric. This is a very active field of mathematics that connects graph theory with many other mathematical areas such as stochastic processes, ergodic theory, spectral theory and several branches of analysis and topology.




From the rise of graph limits theory, two different cases have been mostly considered. The first case is the limits of dense graphs, i.e. when the number of edges of the graphs in the sequence is asymptotically proportional to the square of the number of vertices. This case, where the limit objects are called graphons (from graph functions), is now very well understood thanks to the contributions of L. Lovász, B. Szegedy, C. Borgs and J. Chayes among others [12, 33, 35]. The dense graph limit convergence is metrized by the cut-metric and is equivalent to the convergence of homomorphism densities. The completion of the set of all graphs in this metric is compact, i.e. every graph sequence has a convergent subsequence, which is a very useful property. A shortcoming of the dense graph limit theory is that it has not enough expressive power to study graph sequences in which the number of edges is sub-quadratic in the number of vertices. In fact, every sparse graph is considered to be similar to the empty graph in this metric. An important generalization of this theory are  L^{p}- Lp−L^{p}-graphons [14, 13]. The other case that has been well studied are graph sequences with uniformly bounded degree and the associated notion of convergence was introduced by I. Benjamini and O. Schramm [9] and it has a stronger version called local-global convergence [11, 23]. The limits of such convergent sequences can be represented as objects called graphings. For a thorough treatment of these topics see the monograph by L. Lovász [34].




Unfortunately, for most applications, the really interesting case is the intermediate degree case, not covered by the previously presented theories. Real networks are usually sparse but not very sparse and heterogeneous. For this reason, the intermediate case attracted a lot of attention recently, see for example [32, 30, 31]. In particular, in a recent work Á. Backhausz and B. Szegedy introduced a new functional analytic/measure theoretic notion of convergence [6], that not only covers the intermediate degree case but also unifies the graph limit theories previously presented. This notion of convergence is called action convergence and the limit objects for graph sequences are called graphops (from graph operators). More generally this is a notion of convergence for  P- P−P-operators, i.e. linear bounded operators





 A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega) A:L∞​(Ω)⟶L1​(Ω)A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega)



where  \Omega Ω\Omega is a probability space. As a matrix can be naturally interpreted as a  P- P−P-operator, we obtain as a special case a notion of convergence for matrices. The notions of convergence for graphs are derived by associating to graphs (properly normalized) matrices, for example, adjacency matrices or Laplacian matrices.
In this work we extend the notion of action convergence to multi-linear operators. More specifically, we consider multi-linear operators of the form





 A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r}\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega) A:L∞​(Ω)r⟶L1​(Ω)A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r}\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega)



where  \Omega Ω\Omega is a probability space and  L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r}=L^{\infty}(\Omega)\times\ldots\times L^{\infty}(\Omega) L∞​(Ω)r=L∞​(Ω)×…×L∞​(Ω)L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r}=L^{\infty}(\Omega)\times\ldots\times L^{\infty}(\Omega) is the cartesian product of  L^{\infty}(\Omega) L∞​(Ω)L^{\infty}(\Omega) with itself  r rr times. We name such operators multi- P- P−P-operators.




This convergence notion comes with an associated pseudo-metric  d_{M} dMd_{M}. We therefore say that two multi- P- P−P-operators  A AA and  B BB are isomorphic if  d_{M}(A,B)=0 dM​(A,B)=0d_{M}(A,B)=0. The space of classes of isomorphism of multi- P- P−P-operators equipped with  d_{M} dMd_{M} is a metric space.




We obtain a compactness result for multi- P- P−P-operators analogous to the compactness result for the case of  P- P−P-operators: Sequences of multi- P- P−P-operators  (A_{n})_{n} (An)n(A_{n})_{n} that have a uniform bound  C>0 C>0C>0 on the quantity





 \|A_{n}\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}=\sup_{0\neq f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})}\frac{\|A_{n}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]\|_{q}}{\|f_{1}\|_{p_{1}}\ldots\|f_{r}\|_{p_{r}}}\leq C ‖An‖p1,…,pr→q=sup0≠f1,…,fr∈L∞​(Ωn)‖An​[f1,…,fr]‖q‖f1‖p1​…​‖fr‖pr≤C\|A_{n}\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}=\sup_{0\neq f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{n})}\frac{\|A_{n}[f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}]\|_{q}}{\|f_{1}\|_{p_{1}}\ldots\|f_{r}\|_{p_{r}}}\leq C



for all  n\in\mathbb{N} n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N} have a convergent subsequence in the space of isomorphism classes of multi- P- P−P-operators equipped with the metric  d_{M} dMd_{M}. Moreover,





 \|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|A_{n}\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}\leq C. ‖A‖p1,…,pr→q≤limn→∞‖An‖p1,…,pr→q≤C.\|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|A_{n}\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\rightarrow q}\leq C.



if the sequence is convergent with limit multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA.




We focus on using multi-linear action convergence to define meaningful convergence notions for tensors and hypergraphs.



Definition 1.1.



Let  r,n\geq 2 r,n≥2r,n\geq 2. An  r rr-th order  n nn-dimensional tensor  T TT consists of  n^{r} nrn^{r} entries





 T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}\in\mathbb{R}, Ti1,…,ir∈ℝ,T_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}\in\mathbb{R},



where  i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in[n] i1,…,ir∈[n]i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in[n].
The tensor  T TT is symmetric if its entries are invariant under any permutation of their indices.






First of all, we explain how symmetric tensors can be associated with multi- P- P−P-operators in multiple ways. For example, for a  3- 3−3-rd order symmetric tensor





 T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}} Ti1,i2,i3T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}



we can consider the operator





 T_{1}[\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}]=\sum^{n}_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}v_{i_{1}}w_{i_{2}} T1​[v,w]=∑i1,i2=1nTi1,i2,i3​vi1​wi2T_{1}[\mathrm{v},\mathrm{w}]=\sum^{n}_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}v_{i_{1}}w_{i_{2}}



where  \mathrm{v}=(v_{i})_{i},\mathrm{w}=(w_{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} v=(vi)i,w=(wi)i∈ℝn\mathrm{v}=(v_{i})_{i},\mathrm{w}=(w_{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} are vectors or alternatively








 T_{2}[f,g]=\frac{1}{2}(\sum^{n}_{i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}f_{i_{1},i_{2}}g_{i_{2},i_{3}}+\sum^{n}_{i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}f_{i_{3},i_{2}}g_{i_{2},i_{1}}) T2​[f,g]=12​(∑i2=1nTi1,i2,i3​fi1,i2​gi2,i3+∑i2=1nTi1,i2,i3​fi3,i2​gi2,i1)T_{2}[f,g]=\frac{1}{2}(\sum^{n}_{i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}f_{i_{1},i_{2}}g_{i_{2},i_{3}}+\sum^{n}_{i_{2}=1}T_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}f_{i_{3},i_{2}}g_{i_{2},i_{1}})



where  f=(f_{ij})_{ij},g=(g_{ij})_{ij} f=(fi​j)i​j,g=(gi​j)i​jf=(f_{ij})_{ij},g=(g_{ij})_{ij} are symmetric matrices. These different choices of associating a multi- P- P−P-operator to a tensor give rise in general to different convergence notions for tensors. In the case of  3- 3−3-rd order symmetric tensors the second choice presented seems to be in many cases more appropriate. However, one can require action convergence of both multi- P- P−P-operators  (T)_{1} (T)1(T)_{1} and  (T)_{2} (T)2(T)_{2} associated to the tensor  T TT at the same time.




Recently, in network sciences, a lot of interest has been generated by higher-order interactions (interactions that are beyond pairwise) and the phenomena generated by them [7, 8, 16, 36, 37, 38, 15, 26, 27]. Hypergraphs are the natural mathematical/combinatorial structure to represent higher-order interactions.



Definition 1.2.



An hypergraph is a pair  H=(V,E) H=(V,E)H=(V,E) where  V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\} V={v1,…,vn}V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\} is the set of vertices,  E=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\} E={e1,…,em}E=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\} is the set of edges and  \emptyset\neq e\subseteq V ∅≠e⊆V\emptyset\neq e\subseteq V for each  e\in E e∈Ee\in E.
A hypergraph  H HH is  k kk-uniform if  |e|=k |e|=k|e|=k for every  e\in E e∈Ee\in E.





Limit theories for hypergraphs are much less developed than the ones for graphs due to the bigger combinatorial complexity and for this reason very limited to the uniform and dense hypergraph sequences case. The first contributions on hypergraph limits,[20, 19] by Elek and Szegedy used techniques from nonstandard analysis/model theory to define uniform and dense hypergraph limit objects. This approach using “ultralimits” is well explained in the recent book [44] by Towsner. A more classical approach using “quotients” and regularity partitions obtaining the same type of limits has been developed by Zhao in [45]. The limit objects of this convergence notions appeared earlier in the context of exchangeable arrays of random variables[28, 24, 3, 4, 5, 17]. For sparse uniform hypergraph sequences, a removal lemma is obtained in [43] using again techniques from logic but no limit theory/convergence for sparse hypergraphs is developed to the best of our knowledge. Our hypergraphs convergence based on multi-linear action convergence instead is based on functional analytic and measure-theoretic techniques and can be applied to any hypergraph sequence, also for non-uniform, very sparse and heterogeneous hypergraphs sequences.




To apply action convergence we associate to hypergraphs their adjacency tensor



Definition 1.3.



Let  H=(V,E) H=(V,E)H=(V,E) be a hypergraph on  n nn nodes with largest edge cardinality  r rr. The adjacency tensor of  H HH is the  r rr-th order  n nn-dimensional tensor  A=A(H) A=A⁡(H)A=A(H) with entries





 A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\notin E\\
1&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\in E.\\
\end{cases} Ai1,…,ir:={0 if ​{vi1,…,vir}∉E1 if ​{vi1,…,vir}∈E.A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\notin E\\
1&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\in E.\\
\end{cases}







(possibly multiplied by some normalizing constant) and as already explained we can associate a tensor with different multi- P- P−P-operators and therefore different convergence notions with a relationship between them. These different types of convergence are related to the different types of quasi-randomness for sequences of hypergraphs. In particular, we focus our attention on one notion of convergence obtained in such a way, which we consider being in many cases the most appropriate, and we compare it with the existing notion of convergence for dense hypergraphs (hypergraphon convergence). We underline many similarities in the two theories and we look at some motivating examples that bring us  to conjecture that a modification of action convergence of the normalized adjacency tensor and hypergraphon convergence are equivalent. 




The generalization of action convergence to multi-linear operators allows us to study hypergraph limits and therefore to represent conveniently large hypernetworks with objects that we will call hypergraphops. Hypergraphops are symmetric and positivity-preserving multi- P- P−P-operators and hypergraphs are obviously special cases of hypergraphops. We show that the space of hypergraphops (with a uniform bound on some operator norm) is closed. In fact, symmetry and positivity of multi- P- P−P-operators are preserved under action convergence, i.e. the limit of an action convergent sequence of symmetric, respectively positivity-preserving, multi- P- P−P-operators is again symmetric, respectively positivity-preserving.





We compare the action convergence metric with other norms and metrics in order to better understand this convergence. These comparisons allow us to give several examples of action convergent sequences of hypergraphs and their limits. 





We also study other possible tensors associated with hypergraphs and their associated action convergence. In particular, we present possible choices to obtain meaningful limit objects for inhomogeneous and non-uniform hypergraph sequences. In particular, to the best of our knowledge, we are the first to introduce a meaningful convergence for non-uniform hypergraphs. Covering the case of non-uniform hypergraphs, our limit theory gives us a convergence for simplicial complexes as a special case answering a question in [10].




Generalising the results of [6] is technically challenging as it requires us to use multi-linear operators and tensors instead of linear operators and matrices, for which there are fewer results. Furthermore, this generalisation also significantly complicates the associated notation. However, on a more conceptual level, the main challenge of understanding the limit objects of hypergraphs requires a deeper understanding of action convergence to which we contribute here.




Structure of the paper




In Section 2 we introduce the notation and basic definitions from functional analysis and probability theory. In Section 3 we briefly recall the theory of action convergence and in Section 4 we introduce relevant notions for hypergraphs and tensors. In Section 5 we finally introduce the generalization of action convergence to multi-linear operators and in Section 6 we prove the main compactness result. Moreover, in Section 7 we study several properties of multi-linear action convergence and of the related limit objects.  In Section 8 we compare the action convergence distance with other norms and distances for multi-linear operators. In Section 9 and 10 we investigate how action convergence for multi-linear operators can be specialized to tensors and hypergraphs in different ways and we study the different convergence notions obtained. In Section 11 we point out many relationships between hypergraph convergence notions obtained from action convergence and hypergraphon convergence in the context of dense hypergraph sequences and we conjecture the equivalence of a modification of action convergence and hypergraphon convergence.






2 Notation




In the following, we will denote with  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) a standard probability space where  \mathcal{F} ℱ\mathcal{F} is a  \sigma- σ−\sigma-algebra and  \mathbb{P} ℙ\mathbb{P} is a probability measure on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) (Ω,ℱ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F}). We will denote with  \mathcal{P}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}) 𝒫⁡(Ω,ℱ)\mathcal{P}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}) or shortened  \mathcal{P}(\Omega) 𝒫⁡(Ω)\mathcal{P}(\Omega) the set of probability measures on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F}) (Ω,ℱ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F}). Moreover, we will indicate the expectation of a real-valued measurable function (or in probabilistic language a random variable)  f ff on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) with  \mathbb{E}[f] 𝔼⁡[f]\mathbb{E}[f]. We indicate the (possibly infinite)  L^{p}- Lp−L^{p}-norm of a real-valued measurable function  f ff with





 \|f\|_{p}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(\omega)|^{p}d\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\mathbb{E}[|f|^{p}]\right)^{1/p}. ‖f‖p=(∫Ω|f⁡(ω)|p​𝑑ℙ​(ω))1/p=(𝔼⁡[|f|p])1/p.\|f\|_{p}=\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(\omega)|^{p}d\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\mathbb{E}[|f|^{p}]\right)^{1/p}.



If a measurable function  f ff has finite  L^{p}- Lp−L^{p}-norm we say that  f ff is  p- p−p-integrable (or has finite  p- p−p-moment). We denote with  L^{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},) Lp(Ω,ℱ,ℙ,)L^{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},) the usual Banach space of the real-valued measurable  p- p−p-integrable functions (identified if they are equal almost everywhere) on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) equipped with the usual  L^{p}- Lp−L^{p}-norm or equivalently, in probabilistic language, the space of random variables with finite  p- p−p-moment. We will use a lot of times the shortened notations  L^{p}(\Omega) Lp​(Ω)L^{p}(\Omega) or  L^{p} LpL^{p} when there is no risk of confusion. For a set  S\subset\mathbb{R} S⊂ℝS\subset\mathbb{R} we will also denote with  L_{S}^{p}(\Omega) LSp​(Ω)L_{S}^{p}(\Omega) the space of the  p- p−p-integrable random variables taking values in  S SS.





For a linear operator





 \displaystyle A:L^{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) A:Lp​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\displaystyle A:L^{p}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})

 \displaystyle f\mapsto Af f↦A​f\displaystyle f\mapsto Af

 \|A\|_{p\rightarrow q}=\sup_{f\in L^{p},\ f\neq 0}\frac{\|Af\|_{q}}{\|f\|_{p}}. ‖A‖p→q=supf∈Lp,f≠0‖A​f‖q‖f‖p.\|A\|_{p\rightarrow q}=\sup_{f\in L^{p},\ f\neq 0}\frac{\|Af\|_{q}}{\|f\|_{p}}.



The linear operator  A AA is said to be bounded (or equivalently continuous) if the operator norm is finite. We denote with  {\mathcal{B}}_{p,q} ℬp,q{\mathcal{B}}_{p,q} the Banach space of linear bounded operators from  L^{p}(\Omega) Lp​(Ω)L^{p}(\Omega) to  L^{q}(\Omega) Lq​(Ω)L^{q}(\Omega) equipped with the  (p,q)- (p,q)−(p,q)-operator norm.
A  k- k−k-dimensional random vector is a measurable function  \mathbf{f} 𝐟\mathbf{f} from a probability space  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) to  \mathbb{R}^{k} ℝk\mathbb{R}^{k} and we can naturally represent it as





 \mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}), 𝐟=(f1,…,fk),\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}),

For a  k- k−k-dimensional random vector  \mathbf{f} 𝐟\mathbf{f}, we denote with  \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{f})=\mathcal{L}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}) ℒ⁡(𝐟)=ℒ⁡(f1,…,fk)\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{f})=\mathcal{L}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}) its distribution (or law), that is the measure on  \mathbb{R}^{k} ℝk\mathbb{R}^{k} defined as

we denote by  [n] [n][n] the set  \{1,\ldots,n\} {1,…,n}\{1,\ldots,n\}. In the case of a finite probability space, the law of a random vector has a particularly easy representation. We show this with the following example that will be important in the next sections.



Example 2.1.



Let’s consider the probability space  ([n],\mathcal{D},\mathcal{U}) ([n],𝒟,𝒰)([n],\mathcal{D},\mathcal{U}) where  \mathcal{U} 𝒰\mathcal{U} is the uniform probability measure on  [n] [n][n] and with  \mathcal{D} 𝒟\mathcal{D} the discrete  \sigma- σ−\sigma-algebra on  [n] [n][n]. 3 Action convergence




We briefly recall here, following [6], the notion of action convergence of operators, a very general notion of convergence for operators acting on  L^{p} LpL^{p} spaces defined on different probability spaces, introduced in the context of graph limit theory. Other related works to this limit notion are [25, 39].
We start giving the following definition.



Definition 3.1.



A  P- P−P-operator is a linear bounded operator





 \displaystyle A:L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) A:L∞​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\displaystyle A:L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
 \displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) ⟶L1​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})



for any probability space  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}).
A  P- P−P-operator  A AA is acting on the probability space  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) if the  L^{1} L1L^{1} and  L^{\infty} L∞L^{\infty} spaces are defined on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}). We denote the set of all  P- P−P-operators with  \mathcal{B} ℬ\mathcal{B} and the set of all  P- P−P-operators acting on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) with  \mathcal{B}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) ℬ⁡(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\mathcal{B}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}).





We give here an example that will be important in the following.



Example 3.2.



A matrix  A=(A_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]} A=(Ai,j)i,j∈[n]A=(A_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]} can be interpreted as a  P- P−P-operator acting on the probability space  \Omega=([n],\mathcal{D},\mathcal{U}) Ω=([n],𝒟,𝒰)\Omega=([n],\mathcal{D},\mathcal{U}) where we denoted with  \mathcal{U} 𝒰\mathcal{U} the uniform probability measure on  [n] [n][n] and with  \mathcal{D} 𝒟\mathcal{D} the discrete  \sigma σ\sigma-algebra on  [n] [n][n]. In fact, for  \mathrm{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\cong L^{\infty}(\Omega)\cong L^{1}(\Omega) v=(vi)i∈[n]∈ℝn≅L∞​(Ω)≅L1​(Ω)\mathrm{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\cong L^{\infty}(\Omega)\cong L^{1}(\Omega)





 A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega) A:L∞​(Ω)⟶L1​(Ω)A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{1}(\Omega)










 (A\mathrm{v})_{i}=\sum^{n}_{j=1}A_{ij}v_{j}. (A​v)i=∑j=1nAi​j​vj.(A\mathrm{v})_{i}=\sum^{n}_{j=1}A_{ij}v_{j}.



In particular, a graph can be associated to its adjacency matrix (or its Laplacian matrix) and therefore it can be interpreted as a  P- P−P-operator.





We would now like to introduce a metric on  P- P−P-operators possibly acting on different probability spaces. This means that we would like to equip  \mathcal{B} ℬ\mathcal{B} with a metric and, therefore, with a natural notion of convergence. In reality, we will equip  \mathcal{B} ℬ\mathcal{B} with a pseudo-metric and then quotient over equivalent classes (elements at distance  0 00) of  \mathcal{B} ℬ\mathcal{B} to obtain a proper metric space. We will see that for graphs (adjacency matrices of graphs) this identification of elements is exactly what we want as it identifies isomorphic graphs.
By definition, an element  f ff of  L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) L∞​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) is a real-valued bounded random variable on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}). Therefore, for a  P- P−P-operator  A AA acting on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}),





 Af\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) A​f∈L1​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)Af\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})



is, by definition, a real-valued random variable with finite expectation. Therefore, for functions  f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) f1,…,fk∈L∞​(Ω)f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) we can consider the  2k- 2​k−2k-dimensional random vector





 (f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k}) (f1,A​f1,…,fk,A​fk)(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})



and in particular its distribution  \mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2k}) ℒ⁡(f1,A​f1,…,fk,A​fk)∈𝒫⁡(ℝ2​k)\mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2k}). For a  P- P−P-operator  A AA, if a measure  \mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2k}) μ∈𝒫⁡(ℝ2​k)\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2k}) is such that





 \mu=\mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k}) μ=ℒ⁡(f1,A​f1,…,fk,A​fk)\mu=\mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})



for some functions  f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) f1,…,fk∈L∞​(Ω)f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) we say that  \mu μ\mu is a measure generated by  A AA through  f_{1},\ldots,f_{k} f1,…,fkf_{1},\ldots,f_{k}.
We now define the set of measures generated by  A AA. For reasons that will be clear in the following, it will be convenient to allow in our sets only measures generated by functions in  L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega), i.e. functions taking values between  +1 +1+1 and  -1 −1-1 almost everywhere. Therefore, we define the  k- k−k-profile of  A AA,  {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) 𝒮k​(A){\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A), as the set of measures generated by  A AA through functions in  L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega), i.e.





 {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)=\bigcup_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\{\mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})\}. 𝒮k​(A)=⋃f1,…,fk∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ω){ℒ⁡(f1,A​f1,…,fk,A​fk)}.{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)=\bigcup_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\{\mathcal{L}(f_{1},Af_{1},\ldots,f_{k},Af_{k})\}.

(1)





This is a set of measures. To compare sets of measures, first of all, we will need a metric on the space of measures. For this reason, we recall the following well-known metric:



Definition 3.3 (Lévy-Prokhorov metric).



The Lévy-Prokhorov Metric  d_{\mathcal{LP}} dℒ​𝒫d_{\mathcal{LP}} on the space of probability measures  \mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right) 𝒫⁡(ℝk)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right) is for  \eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right) η1,η2∈𝒫⁡(ℝk)\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right)





 \displaystyle d_{\mathcal{LP}}\left(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\right)= dℒ​𝒫​(η1,η2)=\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{LP}}\left(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\right)=
 \displaystyle\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0:\eta_{1}(U)\leq\eta_{2}\left(U^{\varepsilon}\right)+\varepsilon\text{ and }\right. inf{ε>0:η1(U)≤η2(Uε)+ε and \displaystyle\inf\left\{\varepsilon>0:\eta_{1}(U)\leq\eta_{2}\left(U^{\varepsilon}\right)+\varepsilon\text{ and }\right.





 \displaystyle\left.\eta_{2}(U)\leq\eta_{1}\left(U^{\varepsilon}\right)+\varepsilon\text{ for all }U\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\right\}, η2(U)≤η1(Uε)+ε for all U∈𝒰k},\displaystyle\left.\eta_{2}(U)\leq\eta_{1}\left(U^{\varepsilon}\right)+\varepsilon\text{ for all }U\in\mathcal{U}_{k}\right\},






where  \mathcal{U}_{k} 𝒰k\mathcal{U}_{k} is the Borel  \sigma σ\sigma-algebra on  \mathbb{R}^{k} ℝk\mathbb{R}^{k},  U^{\varepsilon} UεU^{\varepsilon} is the set of points that have Euclidean distance smaller than  \varepsilon ε\varepsilon from  U UU.





The above metric metrizes the weak/narrow convergence for measures. 
We want to be able to compare sets of measures. We, therefore, introduce the following (pseudo-)metric on the sets of measures.



Definition 3.4 (Hausdorff metric).



Given  X,Y\subset\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right) X,Y⊂𝒫⁡(ℝk)X,Y\subset\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right), their Hausdorff distance





 d_{H}(X,Y):=\max\left\{\sup_{x\in X}\inf_{y\in Y}d_{\mathcal{LP}}(x,y),\sup_{y\in Y}\inf_{x\in X}d_{\mathcal{LP}}(x,y)\right\}. dH​(X,Y):=max⁡{supx∈Xinfy∈Ydℒ​𝒫​(x,y),supy∈Yinfx∈Xdℒ​𝒫​(x,y)}.d_{H}(X,Y):=\max\left\{\sup_{x\in X}\inf_{y\in Y}d_{\mathcal{LP}}(x,y),\sup_{y\in Y}\inf_{x\in X}d_{\mathcal{LP}}(x,y)\right\}.







Note that  d_{H}(X,Y)=0 dH​(X,Y)=0d_{H}(X,Y)=0 if and only if  \operatorname{cl}(X)=\operatorname{cl}(Y) cl⁡(X)=cl⁡(Y)\operatorname{cl}(X)=\operatorname{cl}(Y), where  \operatorname{cl} cl\operatorname{cl} is the closure in  d_{\mathcal{LP}}. dℒ​𝒫.d_{\mathcal{LP}}. It follows that  d_{H} dHd_{H} is a pseudometric for all subsets in  \mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right) 𝒫⁡(ℝk)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right), and it is a metric for closed sets.




Moreover, observe that by definition, the Lévy-Prokhorov distance between probability measures is upper-bounded by  1 11 and, therefore, the Hausdorff metric for sets of measures is upper-bounded by  1 11.





We are now ready to define the pseudo-metric we are interested in.
Consider two  P- P−P-operators





 A:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1}) A:L∞​(Ω1,ℱ1,ℙ1)→L1​(Ω1,ℱ1,ℙ1)A:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{1})



and





 B:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2}). B:L∞​(Ω2,ℱ2,ℙ2)→L1​(Ω2,ℱ2,ℙ2).B:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2})\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbb{P}_{2}).





Definition 3.5 (Metrization of action convergence).



For the two  P PP-operators  A,B A,BA,B the action convergence metric is





 d_{M}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A),{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(B)\right). dM​(A,B):=∑k=1∞2−k​dH​(𝒮k​(A),𝒮k​(B)).d_{M}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A),{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(B)\right).







Moreover, we will say that a sequence of  P PP-operators  \left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai∈ℬ(Ωi)}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence if the sequence is Cauchy in  d_{M} dMd_{M}. 





The metric  d_{M} dMd_{M} has some nice compactness properties. In particular, the following theorem gives us that sets of  P- P−P-operators with uniformly bounded  (p,q)- (p,q)−(p,q)-norm with  p\neq\infty p≠∞p\neq\infty are pre-compact in the action convergence metric.



Theorem 3.6 (Theorem 2.14 in [6]).



Let  p\in[1,\infty) p∈[1,∞)p\in[1,\infty) and  q\in[1,\infty] q∈[1,∞]q\in[1,\infty]. Let  \{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} be a Cauchy sequence of  P PP-operators with uniformly bounded  \|\cdot\|_{p\to q} ∥⋅∥p→q\|\cdot\|_{p\to q} norms. Then there is a  P PP-operator  A AA such that  \lim_{i\to\infty}d_{M}(A_{i},A)=0 limi→∞dM​(Ai,A)=0\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{M}(A_{i},A)=0, and  \|A\|_{p\to q}\leq\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|A_{i}\|_{p\to q} ‖A‖p→q≤supi∈ℕ‖Ai‖p→q\|A\|_{p\to q}\leq\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|A_{i}\|_{p\to q}. Therefore, the sequence  \{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is action convergent.





Importantly, we can relate action convergence to the notions of convergence arising in the cases of dense graph sequences convergence (cut-metric, graphons) and uniformly bounded degree graph sequences convergence (local-global convergence).




In particular, consider the sequence of adjacency matrices  A_{n} AnA_{n} of graphs  G_{n} GnG_{n}, and let  v_{n} vnv_{n} be the number of vertices of  G_{n} GnG_{n}. Then,

• 



The action convergence of the sequence





 \frac{A_{n}}{v_{n}} Anvn\frac{A_{n}}{v_{n}}



coincides with graphon convergence [6, Theorem 8.2 and Lemma 8.3]

• 



The action convergence of the sequence





 A_{n} AnA_{n}



coincides with local-global convergence [6, Theorem 9.2].



We refer to [6] for more details.




A lot of properties of matrices and graphs can be directly translated into the language of  P- P−P-operators: self-adjointness, positivity, positivity-preservation and regularity, see Definition 3.1 in [6].




All these properties carry over in the limit if we assume a uniform bound on the  (p,q)- (p,q)−(p,q)-norm with  p,q\notin\{1,\infty\} p,q∉{1,∞}p,q\notin\{1,\infty\}. For the rigorous results, see Lemma 3.2 in [6], Proposition 3.4 in[6], Corollary 2.2 in [25], where counterexamples are provided for when the assumptions of the Corollary 2.2 are not satisfied, and Proposition 3.4 in [6].




In particular, the following special class of  P- P−P-operators is important in graph limits theory.



Definition 3.7.



A positivity-preserving and self-adjoint  P- P−P-operator
is called a graphop.





A graphop is the natural translation in the language of  P- P−P-operators of the adjacency matrix of a graph when we consider the uniform probability measure on the nodes.





4 Tensors and hypergraphs




We start by giving some preliminary definitions and notations on tensors and hypergraphs.





We indicate a vector in  \mathbb{R}^{n} ℝn\mathbb{R}^{n} by  \textbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}) x=(x1,…,xn)\textbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}). For a set  E EE we denote with  |E| |E||E| the cardinality of  E EE and with  2^{E} 2E2^{E} the powerset of  E EE.

where  \Sigma Σ\Sigma is the set of all permutations of  [r] [r][r].





It will be very convenient in the following to consider a tensor as many different possible operators.

We now introduce some notation and notions for hypergraphs.




Given an edge  e\in E e∈Ee\in E, we recall that we denote its cardinality by  |e| |e||e|, and in the following we will denote with  r rr the maximal edge cardinality, i.e.





 r:=\max_{e\in E}|e|. r:=maxe∈E⁡|e|.r:=\max_{e\in E}|e|.



Moreover, we observe that for the adjacency tensor of a hypergraph  H=(V,E) H=(V,E)H=(V,E) on  n nn vertices with largest edge cardinality  r rr its adjacency tensor





 A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\notin E\\
1&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\in E.\\
\end{cases} Ai1,…,ir:={0 if ​{vi1,…,vir}∉E1 if ​{vi1,…,vir}∈E.A_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\notin E\\
1&\text{ if }\{v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}\}\in E.\\
\end{cases}



is a standard notion for  r- r−r-uniform hypergraphs. However, also edges with non-maximal cardinality are incorporated as repeated indices correspond to sets of lower cardinality.




We give here some examples of deterministic and random hypergraphs. We will use these examples in the following.



Example 4.6.



The complete  r- r−r-uniform hypergraph on  n nn vertices is the hypergraph with  [n] [n][n] and such that  E EE is the set of all  {n\choose r} (nr){n\choose r} subsets of  V VV with cardinality  r rr.





Recall that graphs are the  2- 2−2-uniform hypergraphs. Therefore, a random graph is a  2- 2−2-uniform random hypergraph.  We recall here the Erdös-Renyi random graph model.



Example 4.7 (Erdös-Renyi graph).



Consider the vertex set  V=[n] V=[n]V=[n] and we connect each of the possible  {n\choose 2} (n2){n\choose 2} pairs independently with probability  p pp, i.e. following the law of independent Bernoulli random variables. This is the Erdös-Renyi random graph and we will denote it with  G(n,p) G⁡(n,p)G(n,p).





A very common random uniform hypergraph that we will consider is the following.



Example 4.8 ( r- r−r-uniform Erdős–Rényi random hypergraph).



We denote with  G(n,p,r) G⁡(n,p,r)G(n,p,r) the  r- r−r-uniform random hypergraph with vertex set  V=[n] V=[n]V=[n] and with edge set  E EE defined as follows: For every  e ee, set of vertices of cardinality  r rr,  e ee is in  E EE with probability  p pp, i.e. every edge of cardinality  r rr is in  E EE following independent Bernoulli random variables with parameter p. In the case r=2,  G(n,p,2) G⁡(n,p,2)G(n,p,2) corresponds with the Erdős–Rényi random graph  G(n,p) G⁡(n,p)G(n,p).





In the case  p=1 p=1p=1 the  r- r−r-uniform Erdős–Rényi random hypergraph  G(n,1,r) G⁡(n,1,r)G(n,1,r) corresponds with the complete  r- r−r-uniform hypergraph.




We give also another example of uniform random hypergraph:



Example 4.9.



We denote with  T(n,p) T⁡(n,p)T(n,p) the random  3- 3−3-uniform hypergraph constructed taking the vertex set  V=[n] V=[n]V=[n] and as edges the triangles of the Erdős–Rényi random graph  G(n,p) G⁡(n,p)G(n,p) on the same vertex set  V=[n] V=[n]V=[n].





We can generalize naturally this random hypergraph model



Example 4.10.



We denote with  R(n,p_{1},\ldots p_{r-1},r) R⁡(n,p1,…​pr−1,r)R(n,p_{1},\ldots p_{r-1},r) the  r- r−r-uniform random hypergraph on the vertex set  V=[n] V=[n]V=[n] constructed inductively on  r rr as follows:

• 



for  r=2 r=2r=2 we define  R(n,p,2)=G(n,p,2) R⁡(n,p,2)=G⁡(n,p,2)R(n,p,2)=G(n,p,2).

• 



for  r>2 r>2r>2 we define  R(n,p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},r) R⁡(n,p1,…,pr−1,r)R(n,p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},r) as the  r- r−r-uniform hypergraph constructed selecting as edges independently with probability  p_{r-1} pr−1p_{r-1} the sets of  r rr vertices such that  R(n,p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-2},r-1) R⁡(n,p1,…,pr−2,r−1)R(n,p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-2},r-1) restricted to these  r rr vertices is the  (r-1)- (r−1)−(r-1)-uniform complete hypergraph on  r rr vertices.







We notice that the random  3- 3−3-uniform hypergraph  T(n,p) T⁡(n,p)T(n,p) is the same as the random  3- 3−3-uniform hypergraph  R(n,p,1,3) R⁡(n,p,1,3)R(n,p,1,3).





5 Multi-action convergence for multi-linear operators




In the previous section, we have seen how a hypergraph can be interpreted as a tensor and how there are various ways to interpret tensors as multi-linear operators. Therefore, we now want to generalize action convergence to general multi-linear operators.



Definition 5.1.



An  r-th r−t​hr-th order multi- P- P−P-operator is a multi-linear operator  A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r-1}\rightarrow A:L∞​(Ω)r−1→A:L^{\infty}(\Omega)^{r-1}\rightarrow  L^{1}(\Omega) L1​(Ω)L^{1}(\Omega) such that the  \infty\rightarrow 1 ∞→1\infty\rightarrow 1 multi-linear operator norm





 \|A\|_{\infty\rightarrow 1}:=\sup_{f^{(i)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,f^{(i)}\neq 0}\frac{\|A[f^{(1)},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}]\|_{1}}{\|f^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\cdots\|f^{(r-1)}\|_{\infty}} ‖A‖∞→1:=supf(i)∈L∞​(Ω),f(i)≠0‖A⁡[f(1),…,f(r−1)]‖1∥f(1)∥∞⋯∥f(r−1)∥∞\|A\|_{\infty\rightarrow 1}:=\sup_{f^{(i)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,f^{(i)}\neq 0}\frac{\|A[f^{(1)},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}]\|_{1}}{\|f^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\cdots\|f^{(r-1)}\|_{\infty}}



is finite. We will say that a multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA is acting on the probability space  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) if the  L^{1} L1L^{1} and  L^{\infty} L∞L^{\infty} spaces are defined on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}). We denote the set of all  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operators with  \mathcal{B}_{r} ℬr\mathcal{B}_{r} and the set of all  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operators acting on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) with  \mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) ℬr​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}).





We can relate multi- P PP operators and tensors in multiple ways as in the following



Example 5.2.



We can interpret the  s- s−s-action of an  r- r−r-th order symmetric tensor as a multi- P- P−P-operator

for all  i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in[n] i1,…,is∈[n]i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in[n]. We just have to identify the set of  s- s−s-th order symmetric tensors with  L^{\infty}([n]^{s},Sym)\cong L^{1}([n]^{s},Sym) L∞​([n]s,S​y​m)≅L1​([n]s,S​y​m)L^{\infty}([n]^{s},Sym)\cong L^{1}([n]^{s},Sym) in the canonical way.




Remark 5.3.

For functions  f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) f1(1),…,f1(r−1),…,fk(1),…,fk(r−1)∈L∞​(Ω)f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) we consider the  rk- r​k−rk-

We now define the set of measures generated by  A AA. Similarly to the action convergence in the linear case, it is convenient to allow in our sets only measures generated by functions in  L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega), i.e. functions taking values between  -1 −1-1 and  +1 +1+1 almost everywhere. Therefore, we define the  k- k−k-profile of  A AA,  {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) 𝒮k​(A){\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A), as the set of measures generated by  A AA by functions in  L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega).





 \displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)=\bigcup_{f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)} 𝒮k(A)=⋃f1(1),…,f1(r−1),…,fk(1),…,fk(r−1)∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)=\bigcup_{f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)}
 \displaystyle\{\mathcal{L}(f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},A[f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots f^{(r-1)}_{1}],\ldots, {ℒ(f(1)1,…,f(r−1)1,A[f(1)1,…f(r−1)1],…,\displaystyle\{\mathcal{L}(f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},A[f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots f^{(r-1)}_{1}],\ldots,

 \quad\quad=\bigcup_{f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{D}}_{A}(f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1}\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}). =⋃f1(1),…,f1(r−1),…,fk(1),…,fk(r−1)∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)𝒟A​(f1(1),…,f1(r−1)​…,fk(1),…,fk(r−1)).\quad\quad=\bigcup_{f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega)}{\mathcal{D}}_{A}(f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{1}\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}_{k}).



This is a set of measures. To compare two different sets of measures we will use the Hausdorff metric (Definition 3.4) on sets of the space of probability measures  \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{rk}) 𝒫⁡(ℝr​k)\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{rk}) (equipped with the Levy-Prokhorov metric  d_{\mathcal{LP}} dℒ​𝒫d_{\mathcal{LP}} (Definition 3.3)), that we will denote with  d_{H}. dH.d_{H}. 



Remark 5.4.



This is a generalization of the construction in Section 3, see (1). We use a similar notation and terminology to underline the connection with action convergence [6].





We are now ready to define the pseudo-metric we are interested in.
Consider two multi- P- P−P-operators





 A:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})^{r-1}\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{1}) A:L∞​(Ω1)r−1→L1​(Ω1)A:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})^{r-1}\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{1})



and





 B:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2})^{r-1}\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{2}). B:L∞​(Ω2)r−1→L1​(Ω2).B:L^{\infty}(\Omega_{2})^{r-1}\rightarrow L^{1}(\Omega_{2}).





Definition 5.5 (Metrization of action convergence).



For the two  r-th r−t​hr-th order multi- P PP-operators  A,B A,BA,B the action convergence metric is





 d_{M}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A),{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(B)\right). dM​(A,B):=∑k=1∞2−k​dH​(𝒮k​(A),𝒮k​(B)).d_{M}(A,B):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A),{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(B)\right).






Remark 5.6.



This is a generalization of the action convergence metric defined in Section 3. For this reason we use the same notation here. However, this metric can be applied to multi-linear operators differently from the metric defined in Section 3.





As the Hausdorff metric  d_{H} dHd_{H} is bounded by 1, we have that also the action convergence distance is bounded by 1.




We will say that a sequence of  P PP-operators  \left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai∈ℬr(Ωi)}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence if the sequence is Cauchy in  d_{M} dMd_{M}. 





We notice that a sequence  \left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai∈ℬr(Ωi)}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence if and only if for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} the sequence  \left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {𝒮k​(Ai)}i=1∞\left\{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence in  d_{H} dHd_{H}.



Remark 5.7.



The completeness of  \left(\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),d_{\mathcal{LP}}\right) (𝒫⁡(ℝk),dℒ​𝒫)\left(\mathcal{P}\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\right),d_{\mathcal{LP}}\right) implies that the induced Hausdorff topology is also complete [21]. Therefore, a sequence  \left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence if and only if for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} there is a closed set of measures  X_{k} XkX_{k} such that  \lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right),X_{k}\right)=0 limi→∞dH​(𝒮k​(Ai),Xk)=0\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}d_{H}\left({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right),X_{k}\right)=0.





The following lemma is an equivalent of Lemma 2.6 in [6] for multi- P- P−P-operators and guarantees that a subsequence  \{{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)}\}^{\infty}_{i=1} {𝒮k​(Ai)}i=1∞\{{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)}\}^{\infty}_{i=1} converges in  d_{H} dHd_{H} to a closed set of measures  X_{k} XkX_{k} under a uniform bound assumption on the  \|\cdot\|_{\infty\rightarrow 1} ∥⋅∥∞→1\|\cdot\|_{\infty\rightarrow 1} norm.



Lemma 5.8.



Let  \{A_{i}\}^{\infty}_{i=1} {Ai}i=1∞\{A_{i}\}^{\infty}_{i=1} be a sequence of  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operators with uniformly bounded  (\infty,1)- (∞,1)−(\infty,1)-norms. Then, it has a subsequence that is a Cauchy sequence.





This lemma follows directly from the same standard arguments that we summarize here for completeness.




For a probability measure  \mu μ\mu on  \mathbb{R}^{k} ℝk\mathbb{R}^{k} let  \tau(\mu)\in[0,\infty] τ⁡(μ)∈[0,∞]\tau(\mu)\in[0,\infty] denote the maximal expectation of the marginals of  \mu μ\mu,





 \tau(\mu)=\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\int_{(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|x_{i}|\penalty\ d\mu. τ⁡(μ)=max⁡∫(x1,x2,…,xk)∈ℝk1≤i≤k⁡|xi|​𝑑μ.\tau(\mu)=\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\int_{(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}|x_{i}|\penalty\ d\mu.

(3)


For  c\in\mathbb{R}^{+} c∈ℝ+c\in\mathbb{R}^{+} and  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} let





 \mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}):=\{\mu:\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\tau(\mu)\leq c\}. 𝒫c(ℝk):={μ:μ∈𝒫(ℝk),τ(μ)≤c}.\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}):=\{\mu:\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\tau(\mu)\leq c\}.



Let furthermore  \mathcal{Q}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}) 𝒬c​(ℝk)\mathcal{Q}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}) denote the set of closed sets in the metric space  (\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) (𝒫c​(ℝk),dℒ​𝒫)(\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}).



Lemma 5.9.



The metric spaces  (\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) (𝒫c​(ℝk),dℒ​𝒫)(\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) and  (\mathcal{Q}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{H}) (𝒬c​(ℝk),dH)(\mathcal{Q}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{H}) are both compact and complete metric spaces.




Proof.



Markov’s inequality gives uniform tightness in  \mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}) 𝒫c​(ℝk)\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}), which implies the compactness of  (\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) (𝒫c​(ℝk),dℒ​𝒫)(\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) for Prokhorov’s theorem. It is known that the set of closed subsets of a compact Polish space equipped with the Hausdorff metric is again compact.
∎




Lemma 5.10.



Let  A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega) A∈ℬr​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega) and let  c:=\max(\|A\|_{\infty\to 1},1) c:=max⁡(‖A‖∞→1,1)c:=\max(\|A\|_{\infty\to 1},1). Proof.



Let  \{v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v_{i}^{(r-1)}\}_{i=1}^{k} {vi(1),…,vi(r−1)}i=1k\{v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v_{i}^{(r-1)}\}_{i=1}^{k} be a sequence of functions in  L^{\infty}_{[-1,1]}(\Omega) L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)L^{\infty}_{[-1,1]}(\Omega). The result follows as the  1- 1−1-moments of the absolute values of the coordinates in  \tau τ\tau, (3), are given by





 \{\|v^{(j)}_{i}\|_{1}\}_{i=1}^{k} {‖vi(j)‖1}i=1k\{\|v^{(j)}_{i}\|_{1}\}_{i=1}^{k}



for  j\in[r-1] j∈[r−1]j\in[r-1] and





 \{\|A[v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}_{i}]\|_{1}\}_{i=1}^{k}. {‖A⁡[vi(1),…,vi(r−1)]‖1}i=1k.\{\|A[v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}_{i}]\|_{1}\}_{i=1}^{k}.



∎





As in the linear case, for a sequence of multi- P- P−P-operators, we will not be interested only in the convergence of the sequences of  k- k−k-profiles  \{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i})\}^{\infty}_{i=1} {𝒮k​(Ai)}i=1∞\{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i})\}^{\infty}_{i=1} but also in the existence of a multi- P- P−P-operator as limit object. This will actually be the convergence we are interested in.



Definition 5.11 (Action convergence of multi- P PP-operators).



We say that the sequence  \left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai∈ℬr(Ωi)}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is action convergent to the  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operator  A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega\right) A∈ℬr​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega\right) if it is a Cauchy sequence and it is such that for every positive integer  k kk the  k- k−k-profile  {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) 𝒮k​(A){\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) is the limit of the  k- k−k-profiles sequence  \{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i})\}^{\infty}_{i} {𝒮k​(Ai)}i∞\{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i})\}^{\infty}_{i} in the Hausdorff metric  d_{H}. dH.d_{H}. The multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA is the limit of the sequence  \left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}.





Additionally, we will say that a sequence of multi- P- P−P-operators  \left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai∈ℬr(Ωi)}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{r}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is action convergent if there exists a limit multi- P- P−P-operator.



Remark 5.12.



We will often use the following consequence of the definition of action convergence. For an action convergent sequence of operators  \left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} to a multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA and for every  v^{(1)},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) v(1),…,v(r−1)∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)v^{(1)},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega), there are elements  v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}_{i}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) vi(1),…,vi(r−1)∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ωi)v^{(1)}_{i},\ldots,v^{(r-1)}_{i}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) such that

as  i ii goes to infinity.





We introduce now a multi- P- P−P-operator norm for  L^{p} LpL^{p} spaces that is a natural generalization of the linear operator norm



Definition 5.13 (Multi-linear operator norm).



For an  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA the multi-linear operator  (p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)- (p1,…,pr−1,q)−(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)-norm is





 \|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}:=\sup_{f^{(i)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,f^{(i)}\neq 0}\frac{\|A[f^{(1)},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}]\|_{q}}{\|f^{(1)}\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\|f^{(r-1)}\|_{p_{r}}}. ‖A‖p1,…,pr−1→q:=supf(i)∈L∞​(Ω),f(i)≠0‖A⁡[f(1),…,f(r−1)]‖q∥f(1)∥p1⋯∥f(r−1)∥pr.\|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}:=\sup_{f^{(i)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,f^{(i)}\neq 0}\frac{\|A[f^{(1)},\ldots,f^{(r-1)}]\|_{q}}{\|f^{(1)}\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\|f^{(r-1)}\|_{p_{r}}}.






We denote the set of all  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operators with finite  (p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)- (p1,…,pr−1,q)−(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)-
norm with  \mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q} ℬp1,…,pr−1,q\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q} and the set of all  r- r−r-th order multi- P- P−P-operators acting on  (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) with finite  (p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)- (p1,…,pr−1,q)−(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)-norm with  \mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) ℬp1,…,pr−1,q​(Ω,ℱ,ℙ)\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}).




Remark 5.14.



With an abuse of notation, we can think of a multi- P- P−P-operator  A AA with bounded  (p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)- (p1,…,pr−1,q)−(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)-norm as a multi-linear bounded operator





 A:L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)\times\ldots\times L^{p_{r}}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{q}(\Omega) A:Lp1​(Ω)×…×Lpr​(Ω)→Lq​(Ω)A:L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)\times\ldots\times L^{p_{r}}(\Omega)\rightarrow L^{q}(\Omega)



by Lemma 6.6.





The following theorem is the generalization of Theorem 3.6 (Theorem 2.9 in [6]) to the multi-linear case and it states that sets of multi- P- P−P-operators with uniformly bounded  (p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)- (p1,…,pr−1,q)−(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q)-norm with  p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\neq\infty p1,…,pr−1≠∞p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\neq\infty are pre-compact in the action convergence metric.



Theorem 5.15.



For  C>0, C>0,C>0,  p\in[1,\infty) p∈[1,∞)p\in[1,\infty) and  q\in[1,\infty] q∈[1,∞]q\in[1,\infty], let  \{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} be a Cauchy sequence of  r- r−r-th order multi- P PP-operators with uniformly bounded  \|\cdot\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q} ∥⋅∥p,…,p→q\|\cdot\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q} norms. Then there is a multi- P PP-operator  A AA such that  \lim_{i\to\infty}d_{M}(A_{i},A)=0 limi→∞dM​(Ai,A)=0\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{M}(A_{i},A)=0, and  \|A\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q}\leq\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|A_{i}\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q}\leq C ‖A‖p,…,p→q≤supi∈ℕ‖Ai‖p,…,p→q≤C\|A\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q}\leq\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\|A_{i}\|_{p,\ldots,p\to q}\leq C. Therefore, the sequence  \{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is action convergent.





We give the technical proof of this theorem in the next section which is an adaptation of the proof of Theorem 2.9 in [6] to the multi-linear case.

6 Construction of the limit object




For an  r- r−r-th order multi- P PP-operator  A AA and  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} let  cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)) c​l​(𝒮k​(A))cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)) denote the closure of  {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) 𝒮k​(A){\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) in the space  (\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{rk}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}) (𝒫⁡(ℝr​k),dℒ​𝒫)(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{rk}),d_{\mathcal{LP}}).




This section is dedicated to showing Theorem 5.15. This technical proof is a generalization of the proof of Theorem 2.9 in [6] to the multi-linear case (the proof is similar but we have to deal with multi-linear operators and a heavier notation). Let’s consider  \left\{\left(\Omega_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mu_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {(Ωi,𝒜i,μi)}i=1∞\left\{\left(\Omega_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mu_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} a sequence of probability spaces and let’s assume that  \left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} {Ai}i=1∞\left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty} is a Cauchy sequence of  P PP-operators  A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) Ai∈ℬp1,…,pr−1,q​(Ωi)A_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) with  \sup_{i}\left\|A_{i}\right\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c supi‖Ai‖p1,…,pr−1→q≤c\sup_{i}\left\|A_{i}\right\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c for a fixed  c\in\mathbb{R}^{+} c∈ℝ+c\in\mathbb{R}^{+}. For every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}, we can define





 X_{k}:=\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)). Xk:=limi→∞c​l​(𝒮k​(Ai)).X_{k}:=\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right)).



We aim to construct a multi- P PP-operator with  k kk-profile that is the limit of the  k kk-profiles of the operators in a given convergent sequence of operators for every fixed  k kk, i.e. we will prove that there is a  P PP-operator  A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega) A∈ℬp1,…,pr−1,q​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega) for some probability space  (\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mu) (Ω,𝒜,μ)(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mu) such that for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} we have that





 \lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right))=cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)). limi→∞c​l​(𝒮k​(Ai))=c​l​(𝒮k​(A)).\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}\left(A_{i}\right))=cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)).



Before the technical proof, we describe the main idea. For every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} we consider the limit of the  k kk-profiles  {\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i}) 𝒮k​(Ai){\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A_{i}) of the sequence of operators  A_{i} AiA_{i}, which is a set of measures, and we take a dense countable subset of this set. In this way, we have that each point in this dense subset can be approximated by elements in the  k kk-profiles of the sequence of operators  A_{i} AiA_{i}. Moreover, every element in the  k kk-profile of  A_{i} AiA_{i} involves  rk r​krk measurable functions on  \Omega_{i} Ωi\Omega_{i} (in the terminology used before the measure is generated through those functions). In probabilistic language, these functions are random variables, since  \Omega_{i} Ωi\Omega_{i} is a probability space. Very roughly speaking, the main idea is to take, for every  k kk, enough functions needed to generate enough measures (contained in the  k- k−k-profiles of the operators  A_{i} AiA_{i}) to approximate a dense countable subset of the limiting  k- k−k-profile. These are countably many functions for each  i ii. By passing to a subsequence, we can assume that the joint distributions of these countably many functions (random variables) converge weakly and the limit is some probability measure on  \Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{\infty} Ω:=ℝ∞\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{\infty}.
Each coordinate function in the probability space on  \mathbb{R}^{\infty} ℝ∞\mathbb{R}^{\infty} corresponds to a function involved in a  k kk-profile for some  k kk. Since every measure in the  k kk-profile comes from  (r-1)k (r−1)​k(r-1)k functions and their  k kk images, we obtain some information on a possible limiting operator. More precisely, we obtain that certain coordinate functions are the images of some other coordinate functions under the action of the candidate limit multi-linear operator. However, it is not clear that it is possible to extend the obtained multi-linear operator to the full function space on  \Omega Ω\Omega and so we need to refine the above idea.




We now make the above idea rigorous.
We need to work with enough functions to represent the function space of a whole  \sigma σ\sigma-algebra to extend the candidate limit multi-linear operator for the entire function space on  \Omega Ω\Omega. To do this, we extend the above function systems by new functions obtained by some natural operations. In order to do this, we introduce an abstract algebraic formalism involving semigroups. The most challenging part of the proof is to show that, at the end of this construction, the limit operator is well-defined and has the desired properties.




For this construction, we will use the following algebraic notion.



Definition 6.1 (Free semigroup with  r- r−r-multi-operators).



Let  G GG and  L LL be sets. We denote by  F(G,L) F⁡(G,L)F(G,L) the free semigroup with generator set  G GG and  r- r−r-multioperator set  L LL (freely acting on  F(G,L)) 𝑂𝑃𝐸𝑁F⁡(G,L))F(G,L)). More precisely, we have that  F(G,L) F⁡(G,L)F(G,L) is the smallest set of abstract words satisfying the following properties.
(1)  G\subseteq F(G,L) G⊆F⁡(G,L)G\subseteq F(G,L).
(2) If  w_{1},w_{2}\in F(G,L) w1,w2∈F⁡(G,L)w_{1},w_{2}\in F(G,L), then  w_{1}w_{2}\in F(G,L) w1​w2∈F⁡(G,L)w_{1}w_{2}\in F(G,L).
(3) If  w_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1}\in F(G,L),l\in L w1,…,wr−1∈F⁡(G,L),l∈Lw_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1}\in F(G,L),l\in L, then  l(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1})\in F(G,L) l⁡(w1,…,wr−1)∈F⁡(G,L)l(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1})\in F(G,L). There is a unique length function  m:F(G,L)\rightarrow\mathbb{N} m:F⁡(G,L)→ℕm:F(G,L)\rightarrow\mathbb{N} such that  m(g)=1 m⁡(g)=1m(g)=1 for  g\in G g∈Gg\in G,  m\left(w_{1}w_{2}\right)=m\left(w_{1}\right)+m\left(w_{2}\right) m⁡(w1​w2)=m⁡(w1)+m⁡(w2)m\left(w_{1}w_{2}\right)=m\left(w_{1}\right)+m\left(w_{2}\right) and  m(l(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1}))=\max_{s\in[r-1]}m(w_{s})+1. m⁡(l⁡(w1,…,wr−1))=maxs∈[r−1]⁡m⁡(ws)+1.m(l(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r-1}))=\max_{s\in[r-1]}m(w_{s})+1.

In the first technical part of the proof, we construct a function system  \left\{v_{i,f}\in L^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\}, {vi,f∈L∞(Ωi)},\left\{v_{i,f}\in L^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right)\right\},

Remark 6.3.



When functions on  \Omega Ω\Omega are treated as functions in  L^{r}(\Omega) Lr​(Ω)L^{r}(\Omega) for some  r\in[1,\infty] r∈[1,∞]r\in[1,\infty], they are identified if they differ on a set of measure zero. This standard identification of functions allows the correspondence between different coordinate functions. For example let us consider the uniform measure  \mu μ\mu on  \{(x,x):x\in[0,1]\} {(x,x):x∈[0,1]}\{(x,x):x\in[0,1]\} which is a Borel measure on  \mathbb{R}^{2} ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}. The  x xx-coordinate function  (x,y)\mapsto x (x,y)↦x(x,y)\mapsto x and the  y yy-coordinate function  (x,y)\mapsto y (x,y)↦y(x,y)\mapsto y coincide in the space  L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mu) Lr​(ℝ2,μ)L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mu), as they agree on the support of  \mu μ\mu. We will heavily exploit this fact in the rest of our proof.





For the proof of Lemma 6.2 we will need the following two lemmas.



Lemma 6.4 (Lemma 4.3 in [6]).



Let  r\in[1,\infty) r∈[1,∞)r\in[1,\infty). For every  v\in L^{r}(\Omega) v∈Lr​(Ω)v\in L^{r}(\Omega) we have that





 \lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\|v-\sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)h_{j/n,1/n}\circ v\right\|_{r}=0. limn→∞‖v−∑j=−n2n2(j/n)​hj/n,1/n∘v‖r=0.\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\|v-\sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)h_{j/n,1/n}\circ v\right\|_{r}=0.







The following lemma, which is easy to show, see Theorem 22.4 in the lecture notes [18], will be needed in the following.



Lemma 6.5.



Let  r\in[1,\infty) r∈[1,∞)r\in[1,\infty). Let  \left\{v_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\right\}_{i\in I} {vi∈L∞(Ω)}i∈I\left\{v_{i}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\right\}_{i\in I} be a system of functions for some countable index set  I II such that for every  a,b\in I a,b∈Ia,b\in I there is  c\in I c∈Ic\in I with  v_{a}v_{b}=v_{c} va​vb=vcv_{a}v_{b}=v_{c}. Proof.



The first statement of the lemma is shown as follows. The second statement is proven along the same lines as the first one.  \bigcap_{e\in[r-1]}\left\{\omega:\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{F^{r-1}\times[r]},\pi_{(\ldots,l(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}),\ldots,e)}(\omega)=h_{y,z}\left(\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},r)}(\omega)\right)\right\} ⋂e∈[r−1]{ω:ω∈ℝFr−1×[r],π(…,l⁡(f1,…,fr−1),…,e)(ω)=hy,z(π(f1,…,fr−1,r)(ω))}\bigcap_{e\in[r-1]}\left\{\omega:\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{F^{r-1}\times[r]},\pi_{(\ldots,l(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}),\ldots,e)}(\omega)=h_{y,z}\left(\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},r)}(\omega)\right)\right\}



for every  i\in\mathbb{N} i∈ℕi\in\mathbb{N}. To show the third claim, we recall that  \left\|A_{i}\right\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c ‖Ai‖p1,…,pr−1→q≤c\left\|A_{i}\right\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c holds for every  i\in\mathbb{N} i∈ℕi\in\mathbb{N} and thus





 \displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\right. ‖∑j1,…,j(r−1)=1d1,…,d(r−1)λ1(j1)…λ(r−1)(j(r−1))\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\right.
 \displaystyle\left.A_{i}[v_{i,a_{j_{1}}},\ldots,v_{i,a_{j_{(r-1)}}}]\right\|_{q} Ai[vi,aj1,…,vi,aj(r−1)]‖q\displaystyle\left.A_{i}[v_{i,a_{j_{1}}},\ldots,v_{i,a_{j_{(r-1)}}}]\right\|_{q}





 \displaystyle\leq c\left\|\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{d_{1}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}v_{i,a_{j_{1}}}\right\|_{p_{1}}\ldots\left\|\sum_{j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}v_{i,a_{j_{r-1}}}\right\|_{p_{r-1}}. ≤c​‖∑j1=1d1λ1(j1)​vi,aj1‖p1​…​‖∑j(r−1)=1d(r−1)λ(r−1)(j(r−1))​vi,ajr−1‖pr−1.\displaystyle\leq c\left\|\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{d_{1}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}v_{i,a_{j_{1}}}\right\|_{p_{1}}\ldots\left\|\sum_{j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}v_{i,a_{j_{r-1}}}\right\|_{p_{r-1}}.



The sums in the factors on the right-hand side are functions in  L^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) L∞​(Ωi)L^{\infty}\left(\Omega_{i}\right) whose values for the respective  e\in[r-1] e∈[r−1]e\in[r-1] are in the compact intervals  [-\lambda^{(e)},\lambda^{(e)}] [−λ(e),λ(e)][-\lambda^{(e)},\lambda^{(e)}] for  \lambda^{(e)}:=\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\left|\lambda^{(e)}_{j_{e}}\right| λ(e):=∑je=1de|λje(e)|\lambda^{(e)}:=\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\left|\lambda^{(e)}_{j_{e}}\right|, therefore, we obtain that  \sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda^{(j_{e})}_{e}\pi_{\left(\ldots,a_{j_{e}},\ldots,e\right)} ∑je=1deλe(je)​π(…,aje,…,e)\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda^{(j_{e})}_{e}\pi_{\left(\ldots,a_{j_{e}},\ldots,e\right)} is a bounded, continuous function on the support of  \kappa κ\kappa. Therefore, using that  \kappa_{i} κi\kappa_{i} converges to  \kappa κ\kappa weakly and equation (4) again (in particular, integrating the  p- p−p-th power of the absolute values with respect to  \kappa_{i} κi\kappa_{i}), we obtain that





 \lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda_{e}^{(j_{e})}v_{i,a_{j_{e}}}\right\|_{p_{e}}=\left\|\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda_{e}^{(j_{e})}\pi_{\left(\ldots,a_{j_{e}},\ldots,e\right)}\right\|_{p_{e}}. limi→∞‖∑je=1deλe(je)​vi,aje‖pe=‖∑je=1deλe(je)​π(…,aje,…,e)‖pe.\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda_{e}^{(j_{e})}v_{i,a_{j_{e}}}\right\|_{p_{e}}=\left\|\sum_{j_{e}=1}^{d_{e}}\lambda_{e}^{(j_{e})}\pi_{\left(\ldots,a_{j_{e}},\ldots,e\right)}\right\|_{p_{e}}.



On the other side, weak convergence implies the following inequality:





 \displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\right. ‖∑j1,…,j(r−1)=1d1,…,d(r−1)\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\right.
 \displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\pi_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{(r-1)},r)}\Biggr\|_{q} λ1(j1)…λ(r−1)(j(r−1))π(a1,…,a(r−1),r)∥q\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\pi_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{(r-1)},r)}\Biggr\|_{q}





 \displaystyle\leq\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}A_{i}[v_{i,a_{j_{1}}},\ldots,v_{i,a_{j_{(r-1)}}}]\right\|_{q}. ≤lim supi→∞‖∑j1,…,j(r−1)=1d1,…,d(r−1)λ1(j1)​…​λ(r−1)(j(r−1))​Ai​[vi,aj1,…,vi,aj(r−1)]‖q.\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}A_{i}[v_{i,a_{j_{1}}},\ldots,v_{i,a_{j_{(r-1)}}}]\right\|_{q}.



as  \left|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\pi_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{(r-1)},r)}\right|^{q} |∑j1,…,j(r−1)=1d1,…,d(r−1)λ1(j1)​…​λ(r−1)(j(r−1))​π(a1,…,a(r−1),r)|q\left|\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{(r-1)}=1}^{d_{1},\ldots,d_{(r-1)}}\lambda_{1}^{(j_{1})}\ldots\lambda_{(r-1)}^{(j_{(r-1)})}\pi_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{(r-1)},r)}\right|^{q} is a continuous non-negative function. Therefore, putting those inequalities together we obtain the third statement.




To prove the fourth statement, let  \mathcal{H}^{(e)}_{s} ℋs(e)\mathcal{H}^{(e)}_{s} be the  L^{s} LsL^{s}-closure of the linear span of the function system  \left\{\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)},e)}\right\}_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)}\in F} {π(f1,…,f(r−1),e)}f1,…,f(r−1)∈F\left\{\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)},e)}\right\}_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)}\in F} for  e\in[r-1] e∈[r−1]e\in[r-1] and  s\in[1,\infty) s∈[1,∞)s\in[1,\infty).

 \pi(f,\ldots,1)=\pi(\ldots,f,\ldots,e)=\pi(\ldots,f,r-1) π⁡(f,…,1)=π⁡(…,f,…,e)=π⁡(…,f,r−1)\pi(f,\ldots,1)=\pi(\ldots,f,\ldots,e)=\pi(\ldots,f,r-1)

From the second statement of the lemma, it follows that the following equality holds





 \sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)h_{j/n,1/n}\circ\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)},r)}=\sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)\pi_{(\ldots,l_{j}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)}),e)}, ∑j=−n2n2(j/n)​hj/n,1/n∘π(f1,…,f(r−1),r)=∑j=−n2n2(j/n)​π(…,lj​(f1,…,f(r−1)),e),\sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)h_{j/n,1/n}\circ\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)},r)}=\sum_{j=-n^{2}}^{n^{2}}(j/n)\pi_{(\ldots,l_{j}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{(r-1)}),e)},

From the definition of the functions  \left\{v_{i,(t,j,e)}\right\}_{i\in\mathbb{N},j\in[k]} {vi,(t,j,e)}i∈ℕ,j∈[k]\left\{v_{i,(t,j,e)}\right\}_{i\in\mathbb{N},j\in[k]} and the definition of the probability measure  \kappa κ\kappa, we directly obtain the last statement of the lemma.

We will need also the following lemma to prove the existence of the multi- P- P−P-operator. This is the multi-linear version of a classical result about the extension of linear bounded operators defined on a dense set.



Lemma 6.6.



Let  V_{1},\ldots,V_{r} V1,…,VrV_{1},\ldots,V_{r} and  U UU be Banach spaces and  W_{1},\ldots,W_{r} W1,…,WrW_{1},\ldots,W_{r} where, for every  i\in[r] i∈[r]i\in[r],  W_{i} WiW_{i} is a dense subspace of  V_{i} ViV_{i}.
For a multi-linear bounded operator





 T_{0}:W_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r}\longrightarrow U T0:W1×…×Wr⟶UT_{0}:W_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r}\longrightarrow U







 (x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\mapsto T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}] (x1,…,xr)↦T⁡[x1,…,xr](x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\mapsto T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]



there exists a unique multi-linear bounded operator








 T:V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r}\longrightarrow U T:V1×…×Vr⟶UT:V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r}\longrightarrow U






and





 \|T_{0}\|=\|T\|. ‖T0‖=‖T‖.\|T_{0}\|=\|T\|.






Proof.



For every  (x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\in V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r} (x1,…,xr)∈V1×…×Vr(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\in V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r} we define





 T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}] T⁡[x1,…,xr]=limn→∞T⁡[x1,n,…,xr,n]T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]



where  (x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}) (x1,n,…,xr,n)→(x1,…,xr)(x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}) as  n\rightarrow\infty n→∞n\rightarrow\infty where  (x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\in W_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r} (x1,n,…,xr,n)∈W1×…×Wr(x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\in W_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r} for every  n\in\mathbb{N} n∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N} and the convergence is in the natural norm on  V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r} V1×…×VrV_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r}. We show that this definition is independent of the sequence we choose. We consider two sequences








 (x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}) (x1,n,…,xr,n)→(x1,…,xr)(x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})







 (y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}) (y1,n,…,yr,n)→(x1,…,xr)(y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n})\rightarrow(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})











 \displaystyle\|T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]\| ‖T⁡[x1,n,…,xr,n]−T⁡[y1,n,…,yr,n]‖\displaystyle\|T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]\|





 \displaystyle\leq\|T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},y_{r-1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]+\ldots ≤|T⁡[x1,n,…,xr,n]−T⁡[y1,n,yr−1,n,…,xr,n]+…\displaystyle\leq\|T[x_{1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},y_{r-1,n},\ldots,x_{r,n}]+\ldots





 \displaystyle+T[x_{1,n},y_{2,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]\| +T[x1,n,y2,n,…,yr,n]−T[y1,n,…,yr,n]∥\displaystyle+T[x_{1,n},y_{2,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]-T[y_{1,n},\ldots,y_{r,n}]\|





 \displaystyle\leq C\sum^{r}_{i=1}\left(\prod^{i-1}_{j=1}\|x_{j,n}\|\right)\|x_{i,n}-y_{i,n}\|\left(\prod^{r}_{j=i+1}\|y_{j,n}\|\right) ≤C​∑i=1r(∏j=1i−1‖xj,n‖)​‖xi,n−yi,n‖​(∏j=i+1r‖yj,n‖)\displaystyle\leq C\sum^{r}_{i=1}\left(\prod^{i-1}_{j=1}\|x_{j,n}\|\right)\|x_{i,n}-y_{i,n}\|\left(\prod^{r}_{j=i+1}\|y_{j,n}\|\right)





 \displaystyle\leq K\sum^{r}_{i=1}\|x_{i,n}-y_{i,n}\|\rightarrow 0 ≤K​∑i=1r‖xi,n−yi,n‖→0\displaystyle\leq K\sum^{r}_{i=1}\|x_{i,n}-y_{i,n}\|\rightarrow 0



as  n\rightarrow 0 n→0n\rightarrow 0. Moreover,






 \displaystyle\|T_{0}\|=\sup_{x_{1}\in W_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in W_{r},\ x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\neq 0}\frac{\|T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\|}{\|x_{1}\|\ldots\|x_{r}\|} ‖T0‖=supx1∈W1,…,xr∈Wr,x1,…,xr≠0‖T⁡[x1,…,xr]‖‖x1‖​…​‖xr‖\displaystyle\|T_{0}\|=\sup_{x_{1}\in W_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in W_{r},\ x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\neq 0}\frac{\|T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\|}{\|x_{1}\|\ldots\|x_{r}\|}





 \displaystyle=\sup_{x_{1}\in V_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in V_{r},\ x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\neq 0}\frac{\|T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\|}{\|x_{1}\|\ldots\|x_{r}\|}=\|T\| =supx1∈V1,…,xr∈Vr,x1,…,xr≠0‖T⁡[x1,…,xr]‖‖x1‖​…​‖xr‖=‖T‖\displaystyle=\sup_{x_{1}\in V_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in V_{r},\ x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\neq 0}\frac{\|T[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\|}{\|x_{1}\|\ldots\|x_{r}\|}=\|T\|






as the sets  W_{i} WiW_{i} are dense in  V_{i} ViV_{i} and therefore  W_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r} W1×…×WrW_{1}\times\ldots\times W_{r} is dense in  V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r} V1×…×VrV_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{r}.




∎





We now finally define the limit operator  A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega) A∈ℬp1,…,pr−1,q​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega). For  f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F f1,…,fr−1∈Ff_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F, let





 A[\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,1)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,f_{e},\ldots,e)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,f_{r-1},r-1)}]=\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},r)}. This defines a multi-linear operator on the linear span of  \left\{\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},e)}\right\}_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F} {π(f1,…,fr−1,e)}f1,…,fr−1∈F\left\{\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},e)}\right\}_{f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F}. This operator is bounded by the third statement of Lemma 6.2.
Thus, there exists a unique continuous multi-linear extension on its  L^{p_{1}}\times\ldots\times L^{p_{r-1}} Lp1×…×Lpr−1L^{p_{1}}\times\ldots\times L^{p_{r-1}}-closure.
In fact, by the fourth statement of Lemma 6.2 and Lemma 6.6, we get that there is a unique operator  A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega) A∈ℬp1,…,pr−1,q​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1},q}(\Omega) with  \|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c ‖A‖p1,…,pr−1→q≤c\|A\|_{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r-1}\rightarrow q}\leq c such that  A[\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,1)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,f_{r-1},r-1)}]=\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},r)} A⁡[π(f1,…,1),…,π(…,fr−1,r−1)]=π(f1,…,fr−1,r)A[\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,1)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,f_{r-1},r-1)}]=\pi_{(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1},r)} holds for every  f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F f1,…,fr−1∈Ff_{1},\ldots,f_{r-1}\in F.
Last part of the proof: From the last statement of Lemma 6.2 together with the equality





 A[\pi_{((t,j,1),\ldots,1)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,(t,j,r-1),r-1)}]=\pi_{((t,j,1),\ldots,(t,j,r-1),r)} A⁡[π((t,j,1),…,1),…,π(…,(t,j,r−1),r−1)]=π((t,j,1),…,(t,j,r−1),r)A[\pi_{((t,j,1),\ldots,1)},\ldots,\pi_{(\ldots,(t,j,r-1),r-1)}]=\pi_{((t,j,1),\ldots,(t,j,r-1),r)}



we obtain that for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} and  t\in X_{k}^{\prime} t∈Xk′t\in X_{k}^{\prime} it holds  t\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A) t∈𝒮k​(A)t\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A). Hence, for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} we directly observe that  X_{k}\subseteq cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)) Xk⊆c​l​(𝒮k​(A))X_{k}\subseteq cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)). We now want to prove that  X_{k}=cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)) Xk=c​l​(𝒮k​(A))X_{k}=cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A)) for every  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} and thus we still need to show the converse inclusion  cl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A))\subseteq X_{k} c​l​(𝒮k​(A))⊆Xkcl({\mathcal{S}}_{k}(A))\subseteq X_{k}. Let  k\in\mathbb{N} k∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N} and let  v_{1,1},v_{2,1},\ldots,v_{k,1},\ldots,v_{1,r-1},v_{2,r-1},\ldots,v_{k,r-1}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega) v1,1,v2,1,…,vk,1,…,v1,r−1,v2,r−1,…,vk,r−1∈L[−1,1]∞​(Ω)v_{1,1},v_{2,1},\ldots,v_{k,1},\ldots,v_{1,r-1},v_{2,r-1},\ldots,v_{k,r-1}\in L_{[-1,1]}^{\infty}(\Omega). Remark 6.7.



This proof works generally for any sequence of multi- P- P−P-operators with a uniform bound on their order. However, this proof cannot work for sequences of multi- P- P−P-operators in which the order of the multi- P- P−P-operators is diverging.






7 Properties of limit objects




In this section, we discuss some properties of multi- P- P−P-operators that are preserved under action convergence.



Definition 7.1.



Let  A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega) A∈ℬr​(Ω)A\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(\Omega) be a multi- P PP-operator.

• 



 A AA is symmetric if





 \mathbb{E}[A[v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}]v_{r}]=\mathbb{E}[A[v_{\pi(1)}\ldots,v_{\pi(r-1)}]v_{\pi(r)}] 𝔼⁡[A⁡[v1,…,vr−1]​vr]=𝔼⁡[A⁡[vπ⁡(1)​…,vπ⁡(r−1)]​vπ⁡(r)]\mathbb{E}[A[v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}]v_{r}]=\mathbb{E}[A[v_{\pi(1)}\ldots,v_{\pi(r-1)}]v_{\pi(r)}]



holds for every  v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) v1,…,vr∈L∞​(Ω)v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) and for every  \pi π\pi permutation of  [r] [r][r].

• 



 A AA is positivity-preserving if for every  v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) v1,…,vr−1∈L∞​(Ω)v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega) with  v_{1}(x),\ldots, v1​(x),…,v_{1}(x),\ldots,
 v_{r-1}(x)\geq 0 vr−1​(x)≥0v_{r-1}(x)\geq 0 for almost every  x\in\Omega x∈Ωx\in\Omega, we have that  (A[v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}])(x)\geq 0 (A⁡[v1,…,vr−1])​(x)≥0(A[v_{1},\ldots,v_{r-1}])(x)\geq 0 holds for almost every  x\in\Omega x∈Ωx\in\Omega.

• 



 A AA is  c cc-regular if  A[1_{\Omega},\ldots,1_{\Omega}]=c1_{\Omega} A⁡[1Ω,…,1Ω]=c​1ΩA[1_{\Omega},\ldots,1_{\Omega}]=c1_{\Omega} for some  c\in\mathbb{R} c∈ℝc\in\mathbb{R}.

• 



 A AA is a hypergraphop if it is positivity-preserving and symmetric.

• 



 A AA is atomless if  \Omega Ω\Omega is atomless.







In particular, we notice that the  s- s−s-action of the adjacency tensor of a hypergraph is positivity-preserving and symmetric, i.e. a hypergraphop.



Remark 7.2.



The  c- c−c-regularity property of a multi- P- P−P-operator is related to certain regularity properties (i.e. having constant degree) of hypergraphs. In particular, we can consider different notions of degrees for hypergraphs.
For a  r- r−r-uniform hypergraph  H=(V,E) H=(V,E)H=(V,E) we define for  s\in[r-1] s∈[r−1]s\in[r-1] the  s- s−s-degree as





 \deg_{s}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s})=\{e\in E:\ v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in e\}, degs(v1,…,vs)={e∈E:v1,…,vs∈e},\deg_{s}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s})=\{e\in E:\ v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in e\},



for  v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in V v1,…,vs∈Vv_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in V pairwise distinct (compare this degree notion with (17) in the following).
We observe that the  s- s−s-action of the adjacency tensor of an  r- r−r-uniform hypergraph is  c- c−c-regular if and only if the hypergraph has constant  s- s−s-degrees equal to  c cc.





The following lemmas are generalizations to the multi-linear case of the results from Section 3 in [6] for action convergence and the proofs are similar.



Lemma 7.3.



Atomless multi- P PP-operators are closed with respect to  d_{M} dMd_{M}.




Proof.

Under uniform boundedness conditions, positivity and symmetry of multi- P- P−P-operators are preserved under action convergence.



Lemma 7.4.
